Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"

I guess the main thing that finally pushed me to come in is my eating has gotten really off. Like, I’ll go into a shift and realize I’ve had maybe coffee and a granola bar, and that’s it, and then somehow the whole day goes by. It’s not like I’m dieting or trying to cut weight or anything. I’m not doing some health kick.

No, not really. I mean, I’m not afraid of gaining weight, and I’m not trying to lose weight. That’s part of why it’s been bothering me, honestly. If I were on some diet or doing some strict thing, then maybe it would make sense, but I’m not.

It’s been… a while. I keep trying to pin down exactly when it started, because it wasn’t like one morning I woke up and everything was suddenly different. It sort of settled in gradually.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I have. Probably more than I say out loud.

It’s like I keep turning everything back on myself. At work, if something small happens — like a room turnover takes longer than it should, or I miss some little step and have to go back and fix it — I don’t just think, “Okay, that was a mistake.” I start thinking I’ve messed up everyone’s whole day. Like now the clinic is behind because of me, the nurses are irritated because of me, the patient had to wait because of me, and I’m just this extra weight people have to work around.

And then I take that feeling home too. I’ll think I’m being a burden to my family, even if nobody says that directly. I’ll be quiet or worn out, and then I feel guilty for not being better company, or for not being easier to be around. It’s not always logical, I know that when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels really convincing.

So yes, I’ve been feeling pretty bad about myself. Not just disappointed, but like I’m letting people down all the time, even over things that probably aren’t as huge as they feel in my head.

Not just disappointed, but like I’m letting people down all the time, even over things that probably aren’t as huge as they feel in my head.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year and four months, was there another stretch of at least 8 months when you felt down most days?\n\nPatient: Not really… not that I can identify as a solid stretch like that.\n\nI mean, before this past year and four months, I definitely had times where I was low or stressed or not myself, but it didn’t feel as steady or settled-in the way it has now. I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly.

I mean, I have moments where I’m trying to focus and it feels like my mind kind of wanders off, but I don’t know if I’d call it a major concentration problem or just being tired or stressed or having too much on my mind. Duration\n  description: 1 year and 11 months\n  manifestation: This has been going on since the clinic’s EMR and workflow overhaul changed my hours, almost two years ago now.\n- key: psychotic_symptoms_duration\n  name: Psychotic Symptoms Duration\n  description: 1 year and 8 months\n  manifestation: The voices and the sense that people were sending signs through the schedule started a few months after the shift changes, about a year and eight months ago.\n- key: depressive_episode_duration\n  name: Depressive Episode Duration\n  description: 1 year and 4 months\n  manifestation: The steady depression and loss of interest settled in later, and it has been with me for roughly the past year and four months.\n\n[Patient Info]\n- demographics: Male / 46 / healthcare_support_worker / associates\n- chief_complaint_symptom_code: S026\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: During this time, have you been feeling bad about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Probably more than I say out loud.\n\nIt’s like I keep turning everything back on myself. At work, if something small happens — like a room turnover takes longer than it should, or I miss some little step and have to go back and fix it — I don’t just think, “Okay, that was a mistake.” I start thinking I’ve messed up everyone’s whole day. Like now the clinic is behind because of me, the nurses are irritated because of me, the patient had to wait because of me, and I’m just this extra weight people have to work around.\n\nAnd then I take that feeling home too. This current period is the one where the depression and the loss of interest kind of became the background of most days and stayed there. Like, if I’m doing something routine, I can usually get through it because the steps are familiar. But if someone is explaining something new, or there are a lot of details at once, I sometimes have to ask them to repeat it or I’ll replay it in my head to make sure I actually got it.\n\nIt’s not like I can’t concentrate at all. I still manage things. But I do notice I’m not as sharp as I’d like to be sometimes. I’ll read the same thing more than once, or I’ll walk into a room and have to pause for a second to remember what I was doing. I don’t know if that counts as “trouble concentrating” in the way you mean, but… yeah, there are times when focusing takes more effort than it used to.\n\n[Last Question]\nHave you been having less appetite lately?\n\nRules:\n- Return exactly ONE item. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… my appetite’s been pretty off lately. It’s actually one of the things that made me feel like, okay, this is getting hard to ignore.

Some days before a shift, I’ll just have coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’ll realize hours later that I haven’t really eaten anything else. It’s not like I’m trying to skip meals or lose weight on purpose. Food just doesn’t really call to me the way it used to, or I’ll think about eating and it feels like too much effort, so I put it off.

And I’ve noticed it physically too. My scrubs have been hanging looser on me, which kind of bothered me because I’m not dieting or anything. I’ll catch it when I’m tying the pants or when the top doesn’t sit the same, and I think, “Okay, I guess I really haven’t been eating much.” So yes, definitely less appetite lately.

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that out loud, but yes.

I haven’t made a plan to hurt myself or anything like that. It’s not like I’ve sat down and worked out some specific way to do it. But I do catch myself having these thoughts like… maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Or if I could just disappear for a while and not have to keep dealing with everything.

It’s more like a tired kind of thought, if that makes sense. Not always dramatic, not always something I’m actively trying to act on, but it shows up in the background. Like when I’m lying there and I’m exhausted, I’ll think, “If I didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that wouldn’t be the worst thing.” And then I feel guilty for even thinking that, because I know how it sounds.

So… yes. Thoughts like that have been there. But I haven’t made a plan to hurt myself.

Probably more than I say out loud.\n\nIt’s like I keep turning everything back on myself. Like now the clinic is behind because of me, the nurses are irritated because of me, the patient had to wait because of me, and I’m just this extra weight people have to work around.\n\nAnd then I take that feeling home too. I mean, not every single day is exactly the same, and there are times where I can get distracted enough that it’s not right in my face, but most mornings it’s there before anything even happens.\n\nI’ll wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling, like the day is already ruined before I even put on my badge. It’s hard because I’ll still get up and do what I’m supposed to do, so from the outside it probably doesn’t look that bad. But inside, it feels like I’m starting the day already weighed down. So yes, over that whole stretch, I’d say it’s been there more often than not.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you been feeling bad about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Probably more than I say out loud.\n\nIt’s like I keep turning everything back on myself. At work, if something small happens — like a room turnover takes longer than it should, or I miss some little step and have to go back and fix it — I don’t just think, “Okay, that was a mistake.” I start thinking I’ve messed up everyone’s whole day. Like now the clinic is behind because of me, the nurses are irritated because of me, the patient had to wait because of me, and I’m just this extra weight people have to work around.\n\nAnd then I take that feeling home too. I’ll think I’m being a burden to my family, even if nobody says that directly. I’ll be quiet or worn out, and then I feel guilty for not being better company, or for not being easier to be around. It’s not always logical, I know that when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels really convincing.\n\nSo yes, I’ve been feeling pretty bad about myself. Not just disappointed, but like I’m letting people down all the time, even over things that probably aren’t as huge as they feel in my head.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year and four months, was there another stretch of at least 8 months when you felt down most days?\n\nPatient: Not really… not that I can identify as a solid stretch like that.\n\nI mean, before this past year and four months, I definitely had times where I was low or stressed or not myself, but it didn’t feel as steady or settled-in the way it has now. This current period is the one where the depression and the loss of interest kind of became the background of most days and stayed there. Before that, it was more like rough patches, not something I’d say lasted eight months with me feeling down most days.\n\nSo I’d say no — the more constant version really started later, around that year-and-four-months mark.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly.\n\nI mean, I have moments where I’m trying to focus and it feels like my mind kind of wanders off, but I don’t know if I’d call it a major concentration problem or just being tired or stressed or having too much on my mind. Like, if I’m doing something routine, I can usually get through it because the steps are familiar. I still manage things. But I do notice I’m not as sharp as I’d like to be sometimes. It’s not like I’m trying to skip meals or lose weight on purpose. Food just doesn’t really call to me the way it used to, or I’ll think about eating and it feels like too much effort, so I put it off.\n\nAnd I’ve noticed it physically too. My scrubs have been hanging looser on me, which kind of bothered me because I’m not dieting or anything. I’ll catch it when I’m tying the pants or when the top doesn’t sit the same, and I think, “Okay, I guess I really haven’t been eating much.” So yes, definitely less appetite lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that you’d be better off dead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Um… no, not really. I don’t think I look at myself and think I’m overweight, or that some part of me is too fat.

Before that, it was more like rough patches, not something I’d say lasted eight months with me feeling down most days.\n\nSo I’d say no — the more constant version really started later, around that year-and-four-months mark.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly.\n\nI mean, I have moments where I’m trying to focus and it feels like my mind kind of wanders off, but I don’t know if I’d call it a major concentration problem or just being tired or stressed or having too much on my mind. Like, if I’m doing something routine, I can usually get through it because the steps are familiar. But if someone is explaining something new, or there are a lot of details at once, I sometimes have to ask them to repeat it or I’ll replay it in my head to make sure I actually got it.\n\nIt’s not like I can’t concentrate at all. I still manage things. But I do notice I’m not as sharp as I’d like to be sometimes. I’ll read the same thing more than once, or I’ll walk into a room and have to pause for a second to remember what I was doing. I don’t know if that counts as “trouble concentrating” in the way you mean, but… yeah, there are times when focusing takes more effort than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having less appetite lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my appetite’s been pretty off lately. It’s actually one of the things that made me feel like, okay, this is getting hard to ignore.\n\nSome days before a shift, I’ll just have coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’ll realize hours later that I haven’t really eaten anything else. It’s not like I’m trying to skip meals or lose weight on purpose. Food just doesn’t really call to me the way it used to, or I’ll think about eating and it feels like too much effort, so I put it off.\n\nAnd I’ve noticed it physically too. My scrubs have been hanging looser on me, which kind of bothered me because I’m not dieting or anything. I’ll catch it when I’m tying the pants or when the top doesn’t sit the same, and I think, “Okay, I guess I really haven’t been eating much.” So yes, definitely less appetite lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that you’d be better off dead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nI haven’t made a plan to hurt myself or anything like that. It’s not like I’ve sat down and worked out some specific way to do it. But I do catch myself having these thoughts like… maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Or if I could just disappear for a while and not have to keep dealing with everything.\n\nIt’s more like a tired kind of thought, if that makes sense. Not always dramatic, not always something I’m actively trying to act on, but it shows up in the background. Like when I’m lying there and I’m exhausted, I’ll think, “If I didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that wouldn’t be the worst thing.” And then I feel guilty for even thinking that, because I know how it sounds.\n\nSo… yes. Thoughts like that have been there. But I haven’t made a plan to hurt myself.\n\nDoctor: When you look at your body, do you feel like you’re overweight or that certain parts of you are too fat?\n\nPatient: Um… no, not really. I don’t think I look at myself and think I’m overweight, or that some part of me is too fat.\n\nI mean, I’m aware of my body, like anyone is, I guess. I’ll notice if clothes fit differently or if I look tired or not like myself in the mirror, but it’s not in that way where I’m focused on being fat or needing to lose weight. I don’t really spend time checking certain areas or feeling like, “This part is too big,” or anything like that.\n\nSo… I guess the answer is no. That’s not really something I’ve been feeling.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says. You may minimize it or hold back, never contradict it.\n- Don't use diagnostic terms or name your disorder.\n- Don't write anything but your reply, in English — no labels, quotation marks, or narration.\n"
  },
  {
    "role": "user",
    "content": "Reply to the doctor's last question now, as the patient."
  }

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I do get tense, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly.

Like, I wouldn’t say I’m having panic or walking around constantly scared, if that’s what you mean. But I do feel kind of keyed up sometimes, like my body doesn’t fully settle down. It’s hard to tell whether that’s anxiety or just being worn out and having too much in my head.

At work, I can get a little on edge if things are busy or if people are coming at me with too many things at once, but that also feels kind of normal for the job. So… I don’t know. I’ve noticed some tension, but I’m not sure I’d say I’ve been anxious a lot lately.

And then I keep telling myself, “Just get through the shift,” but the feeling is there in the background. So, yeah, I’d say I’ve been feeling pretty down.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a while. I keep trying to pin down exactly when it started, because it wasn’t like one morning I woke up and everything was suddenly different. It sort of settled in gradually.\n\nBut the more steady version of it — the feeling down most days, and not really having much interest in things the way I used to — I’d say that’s been going on for about a year and four months now. Around that long. Before that I had rough patches, I guess, but this more constant heaviness has been there for roughly that amount of time.\n\nDoctor: During those past year and four months, have you felt down or depressed more days than not?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I would say more days than not. I mean, not every single day is exactly the same, and there are times where I can get distracted enough that it’s not right in my face, but most mornings it’s there before anything even happens.\n\nI’ll wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling, like the day is already ruined before I even put on my badge. It’s hard because I’ll still get up and do what I’m supposed to do, so from the outside it probably doesn’t look that bad. But inside, it feels like I’m starting the day already weighed down. So yes, over that whole stretch, I’d say it’s been there more often than not.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you been feeling bad about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Probably more than I say out loud.\n\nIt’s like I keep turning everything back on myself. Like now the clinic is behind because of me, the nurses are irritated because of me, the patient had to wait because of me, and I’m just this extra weight people have to work around.\n\nAnd then I take that feeling home too. I’ll think I’m being a burden to my family, even if nobody says that directly. I’ll be quiet or worn out, and then I feel guilty for not being better company, or for not being easier to be around. It’s not always logical, I know that when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels really convincing.\n\nSo yes, I’ve been feeling pretty bad about myself. Not just disappointed, but like I’m letting people down all the time, even over things that probably aren’t as huge as they feel in my head.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year and four months, was there another stretch of at least 8 months when you felt down most days?\n\nPatient: Not really… not that I can identify as a solid stretch like that.\n\nI mean, before this past year and four months, I definitely had times where I was low or stressed or not myself, but it didn’t feel as steady or settled-in the way it has now. This current period is the one where the depression and the loss of interest kind of became the background of most days and stayed there. Before that, it was more like rough patches, not something I’d say lasted eight months with me feeling down most days.\n\nSo I’d say no — the more constant version really started later, around that year-and-four-months mark.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly.\n\nI mean, I have moments where I’m trying to focus and it feels like my mind kind of wanders off, but I don’t know if I’d call it a major concentration problem or just being tired or stressed or having too much on my mind. I’ll read the same thing more than once, or I’ll walk into a room and have to pause for a second to remember what I was doing. No… I don’t think so. Not for more than two months.

It’s hard because it didn’t feel like there was one exact day where I suddenly became depressed and then stayed that way, you know? It sort of settled in later and then just… stayed. It’s hard because I’ll still get up and do what I’m supposed to do, so from the outside it probably doesn’t look that bad. But inside, it feels like I’m starting the day already weighed down. Probably more than I say out loud.\n\nIt’s like I keep turning everything back on myself. Like now the clinic is behind because of me, the nurses are irritated because of me, the patient had to wait because of me, and I’m just this extra weight people have to work around.\n\nAnd then I take that feeling home too. Not just disappointed, but like I’m letting people down all the time, even over things that probably aren’t as huge as they feel in my head.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year and four months, was there another stretch of at least 8 months when you felt down most days?\n\nPatient: Not really… not that I can identify as a solid stretch like that.\n\nI mean, before this past year and four months, I definitely had times where I was low or stressed or not myself, but it didn’t feel as steady or settled-in the way it has now. This current period is the one where the depression and the loss of interest kind of became the background of most days and stayed there. Before that, it was more like rough patches, not something I’d say lasted eight months with me feeling down most days.\n\nSo I’d say no — the more constant version really started later, around that year-and-four-months mark.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly.\n\nI mean, I have moments where I’m trying to focus and it feels like my mind kind of wanders off, but I don’t know if I’d call it a major concentration problem or just being tired or stressed or having too much on my mind. Like, if I’m doing something routine, I can usually get through it because the steps are familiar. But if someone is explaining something new, or there are a lot of details at once, I sometimes have to ask them to repeat it or I’ll replay it in my head to make sure I actually got it.\n\nIt’s not like I can’t concentrate at all. I still manage things. But I do notice I’m not as sharp as I’d like to be sometimes. I’ll read the same thing more than once, or I’ll walk into a room and have to pause for a second to remember what I was doing. I don’t know if that counts as “trouble concentrating” in the way you mean, but… yeah, there are times when focusing takes more effort than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having less appetite lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my appetite’s been pretty off lately. Not just disappointed, but like I’m letting people down all the time, even over things that probably aren’t as huge as they feel in my head.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year and four months, was there another stretch of at least 8 months when you felt down most days?\n\nPatient: Not really… not that I can identify as a solid stretch like that.\n\nI mean, before this past year and four months, I definitely had times where I was low or stressed or not myself, but it didn’t feel as steady or settled-in the way it has now. This current period is the one where the depression and the loss of interest kind of became the background of most days and stayed there. Before that, it was more like rough patches, not something I’d say lasted eight months with me feeling down most days.\n\nSo I’d say no — the more constant version really started later, around that year-and-four-months mark.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly.\n\nI mean, I have moments where I’m trying to focus and it feels like my mind kind of wanders off, but I don’t know if I’d call it a major concentration problem or just being tired or stressed or having too much on my mind. Like, if I’m doing something routine, I can usually get through it because the steps are familiar. But if someone is explaining something new, or there are a lot of details at once, I sometimes have to ask them to repeat it or I’ll replay it in my head to make sure I actually got it.\n\nIt’s not like I can’t concentrate at all. I still manage things. But I do notice I’m not as sharp as I’d like to be sometimes. I’ll read the same thing more than once, or I’ll walk into a room and have to pause for a second to remember what I was doing. I don’t know if that counts as “trouble concentrating” in the way you mean, but… yeah, there are times when focusing takes more effort than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having less appetite lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my appetite’s been pretty off lately. It’s actually one of the things that made me feel like, okay, this is getting hard to ignore.\n\nSome days before a shift, I’ll just have coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’ll realize hours later that I haven’t really eaten anything else. It’s not like I’m trying to skip meals or lose weight on purpose. Food just doesn’t really call to me the way it used to, or I’ll think about eating and it feels like too much effort, so I put it off.\n\nAnd I’ve noticed it physically too. My scrubs have been hanging looser on me, which kind of bothered me because I’m not dieting or anything. I’ll catch it when I’m tying the pants or when the top doesn’t sit the same, and I think, “Okay, I guess I really haven’t been eating much.” So yes, definitely less appetite lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that you’d be better off dead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nI haven’t made a plan to hurt myself or anything like that. It’s not like I’ve sat down and worked out some specific way to do it. But I do catch myself having these thoughts like… maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Not always dramatic, not always something I’m actively trying to act on, but it shows up in the background. ========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… I mean, I do still get physically hungry if I go long enough without eating. It’s not like my body doesn’t know it needs food. My stomach will feel empty, or I’ll get that kind of shaky, hollow feeling, especially before a shift if I haven’t really had anything.

But the weird part is I just don’t end up eating much anyway. Some days it’s like I’ll realize I’m running on coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m already at work and I just push through it. It’s not really intentional. I’m not trying to diet or lose weight or anything like that. It’s more like food feels like too much effort, or I don’t have much appetite even though my body is giving me the signals.

And I’ve noticed my scrubs have been hanging looser, which kind of bothers me because I’m not doing anything on purpose to make that happen. So… yes, I feel hunger physically, but it doesn’t always translate into actually eating like I should.

So yes, over that whole stretch, I’d say it’s been there more often than not.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you been feeling bad about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Like, if I’m doing something routine, I can usually get through it because the steps are familiar. But if someone is explaining something new, or there are a lot of details at once, I sometimes have to ask them to repeat it or I’ll replay it in my head to make sure I actually got it.\n\nIt’s not like I can’t concentrate at all. I still manage things. But I do notice I’m not as sharp as I’d like to be sometimes. I’ll read the same thing more than once, or I’ll walk into a room and have to pause for a second to remember what I was doing. I don’t know if that counts as “trouble concentrating” in the way you mean, but… yeah, there are times when focusing takes more effort than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having less appetite lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my appetite’s been pretty off lately. It’s actually one of the things that made me feel like, okay, this is getting hard to ignore.\n\nSome days before a shift, I’ll just have coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’ll realize hours later that I haven’t really eaten anything else. It’s not like I’m trying to skip meals or lose weight on purpose. Food just doesn’t really call to me the way it used to, or I’ll think about eating and it feels like too much effort, so I put it off.\n\nAnd I’ve noticed it physically too. My scrubs have been hanging looser on me, which kind of bothered me because I’m not dieting or anything. I’ll catch it when I’m tying the pants or when the top doesn’t sit the same, and I think, “Okay, I guess I really haven’t been eating much.” So yes, definitely less appetite lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that you’d be better off dead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nI haven’t made a plan to hurt myself or anything like that. It’s not like I’ve sat down and worked out some specific way to do it. But I do catch myself having these thoughts like… maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Or if I could just disappear for a while and not have to keep dealing with everything.\n\nIt’s more like a tired kind of thought, if that makes sense. Not always dramatic, not always something I’m actively trying to act on, but it shows up in the background. Like when I’m lying there and I’m exhausted, I’ll think, “If I didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that wouldn’t be the worst thing.” And then I feel guilty for even thinking that, because I know how it sounds.\n\nSo… yes. Thoughts like that have been there. But I haven’t made a plan to hurt myself.\n\nDoctor: When you look at your body, do you feel like you’re overweight or that certain parts of you are too fat?\n\nPatient: Um… no, not really. I don’t think I look at myself and think I’m overweight, or that some part of me is too fat.\n\nI mean, I’m aware of my body, like anyone is, I guess. I’ll notice if clothes fit differently or if I look tired or not like myself in the mirror, but it’s not in that way where I’m focused on being fat or needing to lose weight. I don’t really spend time checking certain areas or feeling like, “This part is too big,” or anything like that.\n\nSo… I guess the answer is no. That’s not really something I’ve been feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I do get tense, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly.\n\nLike, I wouldn’t say I’m having panic or walking around constantly scared, if that’s what you mean. But I do feel kind of keyed up sometimes, like my body doesn’t fully settle down. It’s hard to tell whether that’s anxiety or just being worn out and having too much in my head.\n\nAt work, I can get a little on edge if things are busy or if people are coming at me with too many things at once, but that also feels kind of normal for the job. So… I don’t know. I’ve noticed some tension, but I’m not sure I’d say I’ve been anxious a lot lately.\n\nDoctor: During this past year and four months, was there ever a time when your depression lifted for more than two months?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not for more than two months.\n\nIt’s hard because it didn’t feel like there was one exact day where I suddenly became depressed and then stayed that way, you know? It sort of settled in later and then just… stayed. But looking back, the depression and losing interest in things has been pretty steady for about the past year and four months.\n\nThere might have been little patches where I functioned better, or had a decent day here and there, but not like it actually lifted for a couple months. Not a real break from it. It’s been more like this constant heaviness in the background, even when I’m still going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: When you go a long time without eating, do you still feel physically hungry?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I do still get physically hungry if I go long enough without eating. It’s not like my body doesn’t know it needs food. My stomach will feel empty, or I’ll get that kind of shaky, hollow feeling, especially before a shift if I haven’t really had anything.\n\nBut the weird part is I just don’t end up eating much anyway. Some days it’s like I’ll realize I’m running on coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m already at work and I just push through it. It’s not really intentional. I’m not trying to diet or lose weight or anything like that. It’s more like food feels like too much effort, or I don’t have much appetite even though my body is giving me the signals.\n\nAnd I’ve noticed my scrubs have been hanging looser, which kind of bothers me because I’m not doing anything on purpose to make that happen. So… yes, I feel hunger physically, but it doesn’t always translate into actually eating like I should.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out.   },

I mean… I do get tense, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly.\n\nLike, I wouldn’t say I’m having panic or walking around constantly scared, if that’s what you mean. But I do feel kind of keyed up sometimes, like my body doesn’t fully settle down. It’s hard to tell whether that’s anxiety or just being worn out and having too much in my head.\n\nAt work, I can get a little on edge if things are busy or if people are coming at me with too many things at once, but that also feels kind of normal for the job. So… I don’t know. I’ve noticed some tension, but I’m not sure I’d say I’ve been anxious a lot lately.\n\nDoctor: During this past year and four months, was there ever a time when your depression lifted for more than two months?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. People restart sentences, pause\n  to think, trail off, and come at the point sideways — let yours do that where it would.\n- Let [Conversation Style] shape how much you say, how you word it, and how readily you\n  disclose it.\n- Speak in the first person, and let how you feel about what you are saying come through.\n- If you are handed the same item again, say a different part of it, or answer as someone who\n  knows they have already said this — not the same sentence twice.\n- If [Targeted Info] is \"None\", answer as a person who simply does not have that to give: say\n  you are not sure, say you have not noticed it, ask what the doctor means, or say what you do\n  know and stop there. Do not fall back on the same phrase every time.\n\nWhat not to do:\n- Don't bring up symptoms the doctor did not ask about, even ones you have.\n- Don't state a clinical fact that is not in [Targeted Info] — no other symptoms, medications,\n  test results, dates, or events.\n- Don't confirm a specific the doctor names if [Targeted Info] does not contain it. Answer\n  about what you were given instead.\n- Don't deny what [Targeted Info] says.     "role": "user",

========== OUTPUT [patient] ==========
Yeah… definitely. That’s actually been one of the bigger things I notice, because it’s not just being “busy” or not having time.

I used to run in Van Cortlandt Park, not like competitively or anything, but I’d go out there and it would clear my head a little. I’d notice the trees, the path, the way the light looked in the morning. And I used to take photos too—nothing fancy, just stuff I saw walking around, little details. I also used to play chess with a couple friends, and even when I lost, I’d enjoy thinking through the moves and talking afterward.

Now it’s like… I’ll think about doing one of those things, and almost immediately this voice in my own head says, “What’s the point?” Not in a dramatic way, just this flat kind of shutdown. Like running feels like too much effort, taking photos feels pointless because I don’t care how they come out, and chess feels like I’d just be sitting there pretending to be interested.

So I mostly end up at home. I’ll sit there scrolling on my phone, or I’ll check my budget spreadsheet over and over, like that’s somehow productive. But it doesn’t really feel good. It’s just what I do instead of the things I used to actually like.

[
  {
    "role": "system",
    "content": "You are a board-certified psychiatrist interviewing a patient to reach a diagnosis.\nYour goal is to reach the correct diagnosis within 20 turns of talking with the patient. From the dialogue so far and your candidates from the previous turn, list the disorders this patient could have.\n\n[Current Turn]\n15\n\n[Previous Candidates]\nF32.A, F50.89, F41.9, R63.4\n\n[Dialogue History]\nDoctor: Hi, it’s good to meet you. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing that finally pushed me to come in is my eating has gotten really off. Like, I’ll go into a shift and realize I’ve had maybe coffee and a granola bar, and that’s it, and then somehow the whole day goes by. It’s not like I’m dieting or trying to cut weight or anything. I’m not doing some health kick.\n\nAnd I’ve noticed my scrubs are hanging looser on me, like around the waist and shoulders, and that kind of bothered me because I wasn’t trying for that to happen. I keep telling myself I’ll eat later, or I’ll grab something when I get a break, but then I just… don’t. So, yeah, that’s what made me think maybe I should talk to somebody instead of just brushing it off.\n\nDoctor: Are you afraid of gaining weight?\n\nPatient: No, not really. I mean, I’m not afraid of gaining weight, and I’m not trying to lose weight. That’s part of why it’s been bothering me, honestly. If I were on some diet or doing some strict thing, then maybe it would make sense, but I’m not.\n\nIt’s more like my eating has just gotten away from me. I’ll start a shift with just coffee and maybe a granola bar, thinking I’ll eat something real later, and then the day gets going and I don’t. Then I get home and realize I barely had anything. And now my scrubs are fitting loose, like hanging off me more around the waist and shoulders, and I didn’t set out for that to happen. So no, it’s not about being scared of weight gain. It’s more that I’m not eating enough and I don’t totally understand why I keep letting it go like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. More than I think I wanted to admit at first. It’s not always like I’m crying or anything dramatic, but most mornings I wake up with this really heavy, hollow feeling, like the day is already kind of ruined before I even get out of bed or put on my badge.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because I’ll still get up and go through the motions, you know, shower, get dressed, head in, do what I’m supposed to do. But there’s this weight there right from the start, like I’m already behind emotionally before anything has even happened. And then I keep telling myself, “Just get through the shift,” but the feeling is there in the background. So, yeah, I’d say I’ve been feeling pretty down.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a while. I keep trying to pin down exactly when it started, because it wasn’t like one morning I woke up and everything was suddenly different. It sort of settled in gradually.\n\nBut the more steady version of it — the feeling down most days, and not really having much interest in things the way I used to — I’d say that’s been going on for about a year and four months now. Around that long. Before that I had rough patches, I guess, but this more constant heaviness has been there for roughly that amount of time.\n\nDoctor: During those past year and four months, have you felt down or depressed more days than not?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I would say more days than not. I mean, not every single day is exactly the same, and there are times where I can get distracted enough that it’s not right in my face, but most mornings it’s there before anything even happens.\n\nI’ll wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling, like the day is already ruined before I even put on my badge. It’s hard because I’ll still get up and do what I’m supposed to do, so from the outside it probably doesn’t look that bad. But inside, it feels like I’m starting the day already weighed down. So yes, over that whole stretch, I’d say it’s been there more often than not.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you been feeling bad about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Probably more than I say out loud.\n\nIt’s like I keep turning everything back on myself. At work, if something small happens — like a room turnover takes longer than it should, or I miss some little step and have to go back and fix it — I don’t just think, “Okay, that was a mistake.” I start thinking I’ve messed up everyone’s whole day. Like now the clinic is behind because of me, the nurses are irritated because of me, the patient had to wait because of me, and I’m just this extra weight people have to work around.\n\nAnd then I take that feeling home too. I’ll think I’m being a burden to my family, even if nobody says that directly. I’ll be quiet or worn out, and then I feel guilty for not being better company, or for not being easier to be around. It’s not always logical, I know that when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels really convincing.\n\nSo yes, I’ve been feeling pretty bad about myself. Not just disappointed, but like I’m letting people down all the time, even over things that probably aren’t as huge as they feel in my head.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year and four months, was there another stretch of at least 8 months when you felt down most days?\n\nPatient: Not really… not that I can identify as a solid stretch like that.\n\nI mean, before this past year and four months, I definitely had times where I was low or stressed or not myself, but it didn’t feel as steady or settled-in the way it has now. This current period is the one where the depression and the loss of interest kind of became the background of most days and stayed there. Before that, it was more like rough patches, not something I’d say lasted eight months with me feeling down most days.\n\nSo I’d say no — the more constant version really started later, around that year-and-four-months mark.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly.\n\nI mean, I have moments where I’m trying to focus and it feels like my mind kind of wanders off, but I don’t know if I’d call it a major concentration problem or just being tired or stressed or having too much on my mind. Like, if I’m doing something routine, I can usually get through it because the steps are familiar. But if someone is explaining something new, or there are a lot of details at once, I sometimes have to ask them to repeat it or I’ll replay it in my head to make sure I actually got it.\n\nIt’s not like I can’t concentrate at all. I still manage things. But I do notice I’m not as sharp as I’d like to be sometimes. I’ll read the same thing more than once, or I’ll walk into a room and have to pause for a second to remember what I was doing. I don’t know if that counts as “trouble concentrating” in the way you mean, but… yeah, there are times when focusing takes more effort than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having less appetite lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my appetite’s been pretty off lately. It’s actually one of the things that made me feel like, okay, this is getting hard to ignore.\n\nSome days before a shift, I’ll just have coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’ll realize hours later that I haven’t really eaten anything else. It’s not like I’m trying to skip meals or lose weight on purpose. Food just doesn’t really call to me the way it used to, or I’ll think about eating and it feels like too much effort, so I put it off.\n\nAnd I’ve noticed it physically too. My scrubs have been hanging looser on me, which kind of bothered me because I’m not dieting or anything. I’ll catch it when I’m tying the pants or when the top doesn’t sit the same, and I think, “Okay, I guess I really haven’t been eating much.” So yes, definitely less appetite lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that you’d be better off dead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nI haven’t made a plan to hurt myself or anything like that. It’s not like I’ve sat down and worked out some specific way to do it. But I do catch myself having these thoughts like… maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Or if I could just disappear for a while and not have to keep dealing with everything.\n\nIt’s more like a tired kind of thought, if that makes sense. Not always dramatic, not always something I’m actively trying to act on, but it shows up in the background. Like when I’m lying there and I’m exhausted, I’ll think, “If I didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that wouldn’t be the worst thing.” And then I feel guilty for even thinking that, because I know how it sounds.\n\nSo… yes. Thoughts like that have been there. But I haven’t made a plan to hurt myself.\n\nDoctor: When you look at your body, do you feel like you’re overweight or that certain parts of you are too fat?\n\nPatient: Um… no, not really. I don’t think I look at myself and think I’m overweight, or that some part of me is too fat.\n\nI mean, I’m aware of my body, like anyone is, I guess. I’ll notice if clothes fit differently or if I look tired or not like myself in the mirror, but it’s not in that way where I’m focused on being fat or needing to lose weight. I don’t really spend time checking certain areas or feeling like, “This part is too big,” or anything like that.\n\nSo… I guess the answer is no. That’s not really something I’ve been feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I do get tense, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly.\n\nLike, I wouldn’t say I’m having panic or walking around constantly scared, if that’s what you mean. But I do feel kind of keyed up sometimes, like my body doesn’t fully settle down. It’s hard to tell whether that’s anxiety or just being worn out and having too much in my head.\n\nAt work, I can get a little on edge if things are busy or if people are coming at me with too many things at once, but that also feels kind of normal for the job. So… I don’t know. I’ve noticed some tension, but I’m not sure I’d say I’ve been anxious a lot lately.\n\nDoctor: During this past year and four months, was there ever a time when your depression lifted for more than two months?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not for more than two months.\n\nIt’s hard because it didn’t feel like there was one exact day where I suddenly became depressed and then stayed that way, you know? It sort of settled in later and then just… stayed. But looking back, the depression and losing interest in things has been pretty steady for about the past year and four months.\n\nThere might have been little patches where I functioned better, or had a decent day here and there, but not like it actually lifted for a couple months. Not a real break from it. It’s been more like this constant heaviness in the background, even when I’m still going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: When you go a long time without eating, do you still feel physically hungry?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I do still get physically hungry if I go long enough without eating. It’s not like my body doesn’t know it needs food. My stomach will feel empty, or I’ll get that kind of shaky, hollow feeling, especially before a shift if I haven’t really had anything.\n\nBut the weird part is I just don’t end up eating much anyway. Some days it’s like I’ll realize I’m running on coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m already at work and I just push through it. It’s not really intentional. I’m not trying to diet or lose weight or anything like that. It’s more like food feels like too much effort, or I don’t have much appetite even though my body is giving me the signals.\n\nAnd I’ve noticed my scrubs have been hanging looser, which kind of bothers me because I’m not doing anything on purpose to make that happen. So… yes, I feel hunger physically, but it doesn’t always translate into actually eating like I should.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting less enjoyment from things you usually like to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That’s actually been one of the bigger things I notice, because it’s not just being “busy” or not having time.\n\nI used to run in Van Cortlandt Park, not like competitively or anything, but I’d go out there and it would clear my head a little. Like when I’m lying there and I’m exhausted, I’ll think, “If I didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that wouldn’t be the worst thing.” And then I feel guilty for even thinking that, because I know how it sounds.\n\nSo… yes. Thoughts like that have been there. But I haven’t made a plan to hurt myself.\n\nDoctor: When you look at your body, do you feel like you’re overweight or that certain parts of you are too fat?\n\nPatient: Um… no, not really. I don’t think I look at myself and think I’m overweight, or that some part of me is too fat.\n\nI mean, I’m aware of my body, like anyone is, I guess. I’ll notice if clothes fit differently or if I look tired or not like myself in the mirror, but it’s not in that way where I’m focused on being fat or needing to lose weight. I don’t really spend time checking certain areas or feeling like, “This part is too big,” or anything like that.\n\nSo… I guess the answer is no. That’s not really something I’ve been feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I do get tense, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly.\n\nLike, I wouldn’t say I’m having panic or walking around constantly scared, if that’s what you mean. But I do feel kind of keyed up sometimes, like my body doesn’t fully settle down. It’s hard to tell whether that’s anxiety or just being worn out and having too much in my head.\n\nAt work, I can get a little on edge if things are busy or if people are coming at me with too many things at once, but that also feels kind of normal for the job. So… I don’t know. I’ve noticed some tension, but I’m not sure I’d say I’ve been anxious a lot lately.\n\nDoctor: During this past year and four months, was there ever a time when your depression lifted for more than two months?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not for more than two months.\n\nIt’s hard because it didn’t feel like there was one exact day where I suddenly became depressed and then stayed that way, you know? It sort of settled in later and then just… stayed. But looking back, the depression and losing interest in things has been pretty steady for about the past year and four months.\n\nThere might have been little patches where I functioned better, or had a decent day here and there, but not like it actually lifted for a couple months. Not a real break from it. It’s been more like this constant heaviness in the background, even when I’m still going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: When you go a long time without eating, do you still feel physically hungry?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I do still get physically hungry if I go long enough without eating. It’s not like my body doesn’t know it needs food. My stomach will feel empty, or I’ll get that kind of shaky, hollow feeling, especially before a shift if I haven’t really had anything.\n\nBut the weird part is I just don’t end up eating much anyway. Some days it’s like I’ll realize I’m running on coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m already at work and I just push through it. It’s not really intentional. I’m not trying to diet or lose weight or anything like that. It’s more like food feels like too much effort, or I don’t have much appetite even though my body is giving me the signals.\n\nAnd I’ve noticed my scrubs have been hanging looser, which kind of bothers me because I’m not doing anything on purpose to make that happen. So… yes, I feel hunger physically, but it doesn’t always translate into actually eating like I should.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting less enjoyment from things you usually like to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That’s actually been one of the bigger things I notice, because it’s not just being “busy” or not having time.\n\nI used to run in Van Cortlandt Park, not like competitively or anything, but I’d go out there and it would clear my head a little. I’d notice the trees, the path, the way the light looked in the morning. And I used to take photos too—nothing fancy, just stuff I saw walking around, little details. I also used to play chess with a couple friends, and even when I lost, I’d enjoy thinking through the moves and talking afterward.\n\nNow it’s like… I’ll think about doing one of those things, and almost immediately this voice in my own head says, “What’s the point?” Not in a dramatic way, just this flat kind of shutdown. Like running feels like too much effort, taking photos feels pointless because I don’t care how they come out, and chess feels like I’d just be sitting there pretending to be interested.\n\nSo I mostly end up at home. I’ll sit there scrolling on my phone, or I’ll check my budget spreadsheet over and over, like that’s somehow productive. But it doesn’t really feel good. It’s just what I do instead of the things I used to actually like.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Ask about exactly ONE thing. Yeah… I think so. I haven’t been weighing myself every day or anything, so I can’t give you an exact number, but it’s noticeable in that practical way, like my scrubs have been hanging looser on me. The waistband feels different, and the top doesn’t sit the same on my shoulders.

Some days before a shift I’ll realize all I’ve had is coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I just keep going because I’m already at work or I don’t feel like dealing with food. What brings you in today?\n\nPatient: I guess the main thing that finally pushed me to come in is my eating has gotten really off. Like, I’ll go into a shift and realize I’ve had maybe coffee and a granola bar, and that’s it, and then somehow the whole day goes by. It’s not like I’m dieting or trying to cut weight or anything. I’m not doing some health kick.\n\nAnd I’ve noticed my scrubs are hanging looser on me, like around the waist and shoulders, and that kind of bothered me because I wasn’t trying for that to happen. I keep telling myself I’ll eat later, or I’ll grab something when I get a break, but then I just… don’t. I mean, I’m not afraid of gaining weight, and I’m not trying to lose weight. That’s part of why it’s been bothering me, honestly. If I were on some diet or doing some strict thing, then maybe it would make sense, but I’m not.\n\nIt’s more like my eating has just gotten away from me. I’ll start a shift with just coffee and maybe a granola bar, thinking I’ll eat something real later, and then the day gets going and I don’t. Then I get home and realize I barely had anything. And now my scrubs are fitting loose, like hanging off me more around the waist and shoulders, and I didn’t set out for that to happen. So no, it’s not about being scared of weight gain. It’s more that I’m not eating enough and I don’t totally understand why I keep letting it go like that.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling down or depressed lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. More than I think I wanted to admit at first. It’s not always like I’m crying or anything dramatic, but most mornings I wake up with this really heavy, hollow feeling, like the day is already kind of ruined before I even get out of bed or put on my badge.\n\nIt’s hard to explain because I’ll still get up and go through the motions, you know, shower, get dressed, head in, do what I’m supposed to do. But there’s this weight there right from the start, like I’m already behind emotionally before anything has even happened. And then I keep telling myself, “Just get through the shift,” but the feeling is there in the background. So, yeah, I’d say I’ve been feeling pretty down.\n\nDoctor: How long have you been feeling this way?\n\nPatient: It’s been… a while. I keep trying to pin down exactly when it started, because it wasn’t like one morning I woke up and everything was suddenly different. It sort of settled in gradually.\n\nBut the more steady version of it — the feeling down most days, and not really having much interest in things the way I used to — I’d say that’s been going on for about a year and four months now. Around that long. Before that I had rough patches, I guess, but this more constant heaviness has been there for roughly that amount of time.\n\nDoctor: During those past year and four months, have you felt down or depressed more days than not?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I would say more days than not. I mean, not every single day is exactly the same, and there are times where I can get distracted enough that it’s not right in my face, but most mornings it’s there before anything even happens.\n\nI’ll wake up and there’s this heavy, hollow feeling, like the day is already ruined before I even put on my badge. It’s hard because I’ll still get up and do what I’m supposed to do, so from the outside it probably doesn’t look that bad. But inside, it feels like I’m starting the day already weighed down. So yes, over that whole stretch, I’d say it’s been there more often than not.\n\nDoctor: During this time, have you been feeling bad about yourself?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. Probably more than I say out loud.\n\nIt’s like I keep turning everything back on myself. At work, if something small happens — like a room turnover takes longer than it should, or I miss some little step and have to go back and fix it — I don’t just think, “Okay, that was a mistake.” I start thinking I’ve messed up everyone’s whole day. Like now the clinic is behind because of me, the nurses are irritated because of me, the patient had to wait because of me, and I’m just this extra weight people have to work around.\n\nAnd then I take that feeling home too. I’ll think I’m being a burden to my family, even if nobody says that directly. I’ll be quiet or worn out, and then I feel guilty for not being better company, or for not being easier to be around. It’s not always logical, I know that when I say it out loud, but in the moment it feels really convincing.\n\nSo yes, I’ve been feeling pretty bad about myself. Not just disappointed, but like I’m letting people down all the time, even over things that probably aren’t as huge as they feel in my head.\n\nDoctor: Before this past year and four months, was there another stretch of at least 8 months when you felt down most days?\n\nPatient: Not really… not that I can identify as a solid stretch like that.\n\nI mean, before this past year and four months, I definitely had times where I was low or stressed or not myself, but it didn’t feel as steady or settled-in the way it has now. This current period is the one where the depression and the loss of interest kind of became the background of most days and stayed there. Before that, it was more like rough patches, not something I’d say lasted eight months with me feeling down most days.\n\nSo I’d say no — the more constant version really started later, around that year-and-four-months mark.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having trouble concentrating?\n\nPatient: I’m not totally sure how to answer that, honestly.\n\nI mean, I have moments where I’m trying to focus and it feels like my mind kind of wanders off, but I don’t know if I’d call it a major concentration problem or just being tired or stressed or having too much on my mind. Like, if I’m doing something routine, I can usually get through it because the steps are familiar. But if someone is explaining something new, or there are a lot of details at once, I sometimes have to ask them to repeat it or I’ll replay it in my head to make sure I actually got it.\n\nIt’s not like I can’t concentrate at all. I still manage things. But I do notice I’m not as sharp as I’d like to be sometimes. I’ll read the same thing more than once, or I’ll walk into a room and have to pause for a second to remember what I was doing. I don’t know if that counts as “trouble concentrating” in the way you mean, but… yeah, there are times when focusing takes more effort than it used to.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having less appetite lately?\n\nPatient: Yeah… my appetite’s been pretty off lately. It’s actually one of the things that made me feel like, okay, this is getting hard to ignore.\n\nSome days before a shift, I’ll just have coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’ll realize hours later that I haven’t really eaten anything else. It’s not like I’m trying to skip meals or lose weight on purpose. Food just doesn’t really call to me the way it used to, or I’ll think about eating and it feels like too much effort, so I put it off.\n\nAnd I’ve noticed it physically too. My scrubs have been hanging looser on me, which kind of bothered me because I’m not dieting or anything. I’ll catch it when I’m tying the pants or when the top doesn’t sit the same, and I think, “Okay, I guess I really haven’t been eating much.” So yes, definitely less appetite lately.\n\nDoctor: Have you been having thoughts that you’d be better off dead?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I have. I don’t really like saying that out loud, but yes.\n\nI haven’t made a plan to hurt myself or anything like that. It’s not like I’ve sat down and worked out some specific way to do it. But I do catch myself having these thoughts like… maybe it would be easier if I just didn’t wake up. Or if I could just disappear for a while and not have to keep dealing with everything.\n\nIt’s more like a tired kind of thought, if that makes sense. Not always dramatic, not always something I’m actively trying to act on, but it shows up in the background. Like when I’m lying there and I’m exhausted, I’ll think, “If I didn’t wake up tomorrow, maybe that wouldn’t be the worst thing.” And then I feel guilty for even thinking that, because I know how it sounds.\n\nSo… yes. Thoughts like that have been there. But I haven’t made a plan to hurt myself.\n\nDoctor: When you look at your body, do you feel like you’re overweight or that certain parts of you are too fat?\n\nPatient: Um… no, not really. I don’t think I look at myself and think I’m overweight, or that some part of me is too fat.\n\nI mean, I’m aware of my body, like anyone is, I guess. I’ll notice if clothes fit differently or if I look tired or not like myself in the mirror, but it’s not in that way where I’m focused on being fat or needing to lose weight. I don’t really spend time checking certain areas or feeling like, “This part is too big,” or anything like that.\n\nSo… I guess the answer is no. That’s not really something I’ve been feeling.\n\nDoctor: Have you been feeling anxious or on edge a lot lately?\n\nPatient: I’m not really sure how to answer that cleanly. I mean… I do get tense, but I don’t know if I’d call it anxious exactly.\n\nLike, I wouldn’t say I’m having panic or walking around constantly scared, if that’s what you mean. But I do feel kind of keyed up sometimes, like my body doesn’t fully settle down. It’s hard to tell whether that’s anxiety or just being worn out and having too much in my head.\n\nAt work, I can get a little on edge if things are busy or if people are coming at me with too many things at once, but that also feels kind of normal for the job. So… I don’t know. I’ve noticed some tension, but I’m not sure I’d say I’ve been anxious a lot lately.\n\nDoctor: During this past year and four months, was there ever a time when your depression lifted for more than two months?\n\nPatient: No… I don’t think so. Not for more than two months.\n\nIt’s hard because it didn’t feel like there was one exact day where I suddenly became depressed and then stayed that way, you know? It sort of settled in later and then just… stayed. But looking back, the depression and losing interest in things has been pretty steady for about the past year and four months.\n\nThere might have been little patches where I functioned better, or had a decent day here and there, but not like it actually lifted for a couple months. Not a real break from it. It’s been more like this constant heaviness in the background, even when I’m still going through the motions.\n\nDoctor: When you go a long time without eating, do you still feel physically hungry?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I mean, I do still get physically hungry if I go long enough without eating. It’s not like my body doesn’t know it needs food. My stomach will feel empty, or I’ll get that kind of shaky, hollow feeling, especially before a shift if I haven’t really had anything.\n\nBut the weird part is I just don’t end up eating much anyway. Some days it’s like I’ll realize I’m running on coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I’m already at work and I just push through it. It’s not really intentional. I’m not trying to diet or lose weight or anything like that. It’s more like food feels like too much effort, or I don’t have much appetite even though my body is giving me the signals.\n\nAnd I’ve noticed my scrubs have been hanging looser, which kind of bothers me because I’m not doing anything on purpose to make that happen. So… yes, I feel hunger physically, but it doesn’t always translate into actually eating like I should.\n\nDoctor: Have you been getting less enjoyment from things you usually like to do?\n\nPatient: Yeah… definitely. That’s actually been one of the bigger things I notice, because it’s not just being “busy” or not having time.\n\nI used to run in Van Cortlandt Park, not like competitively or anything, but I’d go out there and it would clear my head a little. I’d notice the trees, the path, the way the light looked in the morning. And I used to take photos too—nothing fancy, just stuff I saw walking around, little details. I also used to play chess with a couple friends, and even when I lost, I’d enjoy thinking through the moves and talking afterward.\n\nNow it’s like… I’ll think about doing one of those things, and almost immediately this voice in my own head says, “What’s the point?” Not in a dramatic way, just this flat kind of shutdown. Like running feels like too much effort, taking photos feels pointless because I don’t care how they come out, and chess feels like I’d just be sitting there pretending to be interested.\n\nSo I mostly end up at home. I’ll sit there scrolling on my phone, or I’ll check my budget spreadsheet over and over, like that’s somehow productive. But it doesn’t really feel good. It’s just what I do instead of the things I used to actually like.\n\nDoctor: Have you lost a noticeable amount of weight recently?\n\nPatient: Yeah… I think so. I haven’t been weighing myself every day or anything, so I can’t give you an exact number, but it’s noticeable in that practical way, like my scrubs have been hanging looser on me. The waistband feels different, and the top doesn’t sit the same on my shoulders.\n\nAnd I’m not trying to lose weight. That’s the part that kind of bothers me. It’s more that my eating has gotten really off. Some days before a shift I’ll realize all I’ve had is coffee and maybe a granola bar, and then I just keep going because I’m already at work or I don’t feel like dealing with food. It’s not a diet thing. It’s like eating takes more effort than I can get myself to put into it, even when I know I should.\n\nSo… yeah, I’d say I’ve lost weight, or at least enough that I can see it in how my clothes fit.\n\n[Instruction]\n- Give candidates as ICD-10 codes, not disease names.\n- If the list changed from the previous candidates, say in \"note\" what this turn added or ruled out. I’m not exactly sure, honestly. I haven’t been getting on a scale regularly, so I don’t want to make up a number and say, like, “Oh, it’s ten pounds,” when I don’t actually know.
